People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

a suicide attempt.\n  manifestation: On the worst nights I think it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up, but I haven’t made a plan to hurt myself.\n- key: S033\n  name: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\n  description: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.\n  manifestation: There are periods when I feel bright and unstoppable and then suddenly furious over something small, like my husband moving my paints or questioning a receipt.\n- key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: During those energized times I can’t sit still, so I clean shelves, rearrange art supplies, draft poems, and pace through the garden as if stopping would make me crawl out of my skin.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).\n  manifestation: I’ll become sure that my art-therapy workshop is going to change people’s lives in a major way, even though I haven’t finished the training yet.\n- key: S036\n  name: Decreased_Sleep\n  description: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).\n  manifestation: I can sleep three or four hours and wake up feeling charged, ready to write blog posts or plan projects before the sun is even up.\n- key: S038\n  name: Racing_Thoughts\n  description: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.\n  manifestation: My thoughts sometimes run so fast that one idea bumps into the next—roses, money, colors, messages, classes—and I can’t slow them down enough to explain them.\n- key: S039\n  name: Manic_Distractibility\n  description: Attention is additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: psychotic_symptoms and manic_episode occur concurrently\n  manifestation: When I’m in those charged, unstoppable stretches description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve fallen behind on my facilitator training and private blog work because my projects and explanations became hard to organize, and the tension at home has It just sort of hits me.\n\nDoctor: When you say you go through these stretches of feeling down and empty, how long do these periods typically last? Are we talking days, weeks, or months?\n\nPatient: Well, if I look back over the last year or so, those gray periods... the times where I just feel heavy and guilty and completely lose interest in doing anything... they've added up to about seven months total. It's usually weeks or sometimes months at a time before it lifts.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year when you've been feeling down and gray, have you had other times in your life — maybe years ago — when you felt this same kind of heavy, empty depression that lasted for weeks or months?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I've had normal bad days like anyone else, but nothing like this heavy, empty feeling that just drags on for weeks. This past year is the first time it's been this bad and lasted this long.\n\nDoctor: During this past year when you've been having these heavy, empty periods, have there been times in between when you felt unusually energetic, needed less sleep than normal, or felt unusually good or 'up' — maybe times when you were more talkative or had racing thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, there are times like that. In between those heavy, gray periods, I'll go through stretches where I feel... well, just really bright and unstoppable. Like I can do anything. \n\nBut it's kind of a mixed feeling, because during those same times, I can get suddenly furious over something really small. Like, if my husband moves my paints, or if he questions a receipt from the store, I just snap and get incredibly mad out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you feel really bright and unstoppable, how much sleep are you actually getting? Do you need less sleep than usual, or are you sleeping the same amount but just not feeling tired?\n\nPatient: I definitely need a lot less sleep during those times. Um, I can sleep for maybe three or four hours and wake up feeling completely charged. I don't feel tired at all. I'm just ready to go, ready to write blog posts or plan out new projects before the sun is even up.\n\nDoctor: When you're in those bright, unstoppable periods where you need only a few hours of sleep and feel charged up — how long do those stretches typically last? Are we talking about a couple of days, several days, a week or more?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm not really sure exactly how long they last. I haven't really kept track of the days. I just know it happens, but I couldn't tell you if it's a few days or a week or what.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those bright, unstoppable periods with just a few hours of sleep, do you think you typically go through at least 4 or 5 days like that, or is it usually shorter than that?\n\nPatient: I honestly couldn't say for sure. I just don't pay attention to the calendar when I feel like that. I'm so busy doing things and feeling good that I don't stop to count how many days it's been. It could be four or five days, or it might be shorter, I just really don't know.\n\nDoctor: I understand you don't track the calendar closely during those bright, energetic periods. But thinking back to the last few times this happened, would you say that each time you felt that way, it lasted at least several days — like, you'd wake up feeling that way for multiple mornings in a row?\n\nPatient: I know you're trying to figure out the exact timeframe, but... I really just don't remember it that way. When I'm in that zone, time just kind of blurs together. Um, it might be multiple mornings in a row, or it might just be a couple before things shift. I really just don't have a clear memory of how long it actually stretches out.\n\nDoctor: During those bright, energetic periods where you need very little sleep and feel charged up, do you think people around you — like your husband or family — would have noticed a clear change in your behavior for at least a few days at a time, or does it seem more like just a few hours or a single day when you're like that?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know what my husband or family would say. You'd probably have to ask them. I just don't know what it looks like from their side.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those bright, energetic periods, do you find that you're still feeling that way and needing very little sleep when you wake up the next morning, and then the morning after that?\n\nPatient: I just... I can't really say for sure. Like I said, the days kind of just run together when I feel like that. I might wake up feeling that way the next morning, or the morning after that, but I just don't have a clear memory of it. It's hard to separate one day from the next when I'm in that headspace.\n\nDoctor: Think back to the most recent time you felt that bright, unstoppable, charged-up feeling with very little sleep needed. This past year is the first time it's been this bad and lasted this long.\n\nDoctor: During this past year when you've been having these heavy, empty periods, have there been times in between when you felt unusually energetic, needed less sleep than normal, or felt unusually good or 'up' — maybe times when you were more talkative or had racing thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, there are times like that. In between those heavy, gray periods, I'll go through stretches where I feel... well, just really bright and unstoppable. Like I can do anything. \n\nBut it's kind of a mixed feeling, because during those same times, I can get suddenly furious over something really small. Like, if my husband moves my paints, or if he questions a receipt from the store, I just snap and get incredibly mad out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you feel really bright and unstoppable, how much sleep are you actually getting? Do you need less sleep than usual, or are you sleeping the same amount but just not feeling tired?\n\nPatient: I definitely need a lot less sleep during those times. Um, I can sleep for maybe three or four hours and wake up feeling completely charged. I don't feel tired at all. I'm just ready to go, ready to write blog posts or plan out new projects before the sun is even up.\n\nDoctor: When you're in those bright, unstoppable periods where you need only a few hours of sleep and feel charged up — how long do those stretches typically last? Are we talking about a couple of days, several days, a week or more?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm not really sure exactly how long they last. I haven't really kept track of the days. I just know it happens, but I couldn't tell you if it's a few days or a week or what.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those bright, unstoppable periods with just a few hours of sleep, do you think you typically go through at least 4 or 5 days like that, or is it usually shorter than that?\n\nPatient: I honestly couldn't say for sure. I just don't pay attention to the calendar when I feel like that. I'm so busy doing things and feeling good that I don't stop to count how many days it's been. It could be four or five days, or it might be shorter, I just really don't know.\n\nDoctor: I understand you don't track the calendar closely during those bright, energetic periods. But thinking back to the last few times this happened, would you say that each time you felt that way, it lasted at least several days — like, you'd wake up feeling that way for multiple mornings in a row?\n\nPatient: I know you're trying to figure out the exact timeframe, but... I really just don't remember it that way. When I'm in that zone, time just kind of blurs together. Um, it might be multiple mornings in a row, or it might just be a couple before things shift. I really just don't have a clear memory of how long it actually stretches out.\n\nDoctor: During those bright, energetic periods where you need very little sleep and feel charged up, do you think people around you — like your husband or family — would have noticed a clear change in your behavior for at least a few days at a time, or does it seem more like just a few hours or a single day when you're like that?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know what my husband or family would say. You'd probably have to ask them. I mean, they might notice I'm doing more things around the house or talking a bit faster, but I couldn't tell you if they think it lasts for a few days or just a day. I just don't know what it looks like from their side.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those bright, energetic periods, do you find that you're still feeling that way and needing very little sleep when you wake up the next morning, and then the morning after that?\n\nPatient: I just... I can't really say for sure. Like I said, the days kind of just run together when I feel like that. I might wake up feeling that way the next morning, or the morning after that, but I just don't have a clear memory of it. I really just can't give you one. Even thinking about the very last time it happened, I honestly don't know. I just can't put a start and end date on it. It could have been three days, or maybe a week, but... I just don't have a way to measure it when I'm in it. When I get like that, I'm doing a lot more than usual. I'm just constantly moving and starting things. But... like I mentioned earlier, the days just kind of blur together for me when I get like that. I might still be going strong when I go to bed on the third night, or maybe it starts to fade before then. I just don't keep track of the time well enough to tell you for sure if it's still happening by night three.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those bright, energetic periods where you're doing all those extra activities and sleeping very little, would your husband or someone close to you say they noticed you acting that way for at least three or four days in a row, or does it seem like it would be less than that?\n\nPatient: I've never actually asked my husband to count the days, so... I really don't know what he would say. He definitely notices when I get into those moods because I'm doing so much around the house, but I'm not sure if he'd say it lasts three or four days in a row, or if it's shorter than that. We just haven't ever talked about it in terms of an exact timeframe.\n\nDoctor: Let me ask it a different way. When you're in one of those bright, energetic periods, is there a point where you wake up and think, 'Oh, I'm back to normal now,' or do you find that you keep waking up feeling that same energetic, charged-up way for several days before it finally shifts?\n\nPatient: I know you're trying to pin down the timeframe, but... I really just don't know. I don't think there's a specific morning where I wake up and think, 'Okay, I'm back to normal.' It's not like a switch flips that I notice right away. \n\nI'm just going and going, and then eventually... I'm not. But I couldn't tell you if I keep waking up feeling that charged-up way for several days before it finally shifts. I just don't really pay attention to it like that while it's happening.\n\nDoctor: I know the timing is hard to pin down, but let me ask this way: On a scale where 1 is 'definitely less than 3 days' and 5 is 'definitely 4 or more days in a row' — where would you place those bright, energetic periods where you're sleeping very little and doing all those extra activities? Which number feels closest to what you remember?\n\nPatient: I know you're trying to give me a way to measure it with that scale, but... I really just can't pick a number for you. \n\nBecause I honestly don't know if it's a 1, or a 5, or something in between. Like I've been saying, I just don't have a clear memory of how many days those periods actually last when I'm in them. I would just be completely guessing if I picked a number, and I don't want to tell you the wrong thing. I just really don't know.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It's usually weeks or sometimes months at a time before it lifts.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year when you've been feeling down and gray, have you had other times in your life — maybe years ago — when you felt this same kind of heavy, empty depression that lasted for weeks or months?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I've had normal bad days like anyone else, but nothing like this heavy, empty feeling that just drags on for weeks. This past year is the first time it's been this bad and lasted this long.\n\nDoctor: During this past year when you've been having these heavy, empty periods, have there been times in between when you felt unusually energetic, needed less sleep than normal, or felt unusually good or 'up' — maybe times when you were more talkative or had racing thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, there are times like that. In between those heavy, gray periods, I'll go through stretches where I feel... well, just really bright and unstoppable. Like I can do anything. \n\nBut it's kind of a mixed feeling, because during those same times, I can get suddenly furious over something really small. Like, if my husband moves my paints, or if he questions a receipt from the store, I just snap and get incredibly mad out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you feel really bright and unstoppable, how much sleep are you actually getting? Do you need less sleep than usual, or are you sleeping the same amount but just not feeling tired?\n\nPatient: I definitely need a lot less sleep during those times. Um, I can sleep for maybe three or four hours and wake up feeling completely charged. I don't feel tired at all. I'm just ready to go, ready to write blog posts or plan out new projects before the sun is even up.\n\nDoctor: When you're in those bright, unstoppable periods where you need only a few hours of sleep and feel charged up — how long do those stretches typically last? Are we talking about a couple of days, several days, a week or more?\n\nPatient: Um, I'm not really sure exactly how long they last. I haven't really kept track of the days. I just know it happens, but I couldn't tell you if it's a few days or a week or what.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those bright, unstoppable periods with just a few hours of sleep, do you think you typically go through at least 4 or 5 days like that, or is it usually shorter than that?\n\nPatient: I honestly couldn't say for sure. I just don't pay attention to the calendar when I feel like that. I'm so busy doing things and feeling good that I don't stop to count how many days it's been. It could be four or five days, or it might be shorter, I just really don't know.\n\nDoctor: I understand you don't track the calendar closely during those bright, energetic periods. But thinking back to the last few times this happened, would you say that each time you felt that way, it lasted at least several days — like, you'd wake up feeling that way for multiple mornings in a row?\n\nPatient: I know you're trying to figure out the exact timeframe, but... I really just don't remember it that way. When I'm in that zone, time just kind of blurs together. Um, it might be multiple mornings in a row, or it might just be a couple before things shift. I really just don't have a clear memory of how long it actually stretches out.\n\nDoctor: During those bright, energetic periods where you need very little sleep and feel charged up, do you think people around you — like your husband or family — would have noticed a clear change in your behavior for at least a few days at a time, or does it seem more like just a few hours or a single day when you're like that?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know what my husband or family would say. You'd probably have to ask them. I mean, they might notice I'm doing more things around the house or talking a bit faster, but I couldn't tell you if they think it lasts for a few days or just a day. I just don't know what it looks like from their side.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those bright, energetic periods, do you find that you're still feeling that way and needing very little sleep when you wake up the next morning, and then the morning after that?\n\nPatient: I just... I can't really say for sure. Like I said, the days kind of just run together when I feel like that. I might wake up feeling that way the next morning, or the morning after that, but I just don't have a clear memory of it. It's hard to separate one day from the next when I'm in that headspace.\n\nDoctor: Think back to the most recent time you felt that bright, unstoppable, charged-up feeling with very little sleep needed. From the day it started to the day it ended, roughly how many days would you say that lasted?\n\nPatient: I know you're really trying to get a specific number of days from me, but I... I really just can't give you one. Even thinking about the very last time it happened, I honestly don't know. I just can't put a start and end date on it. It could have been three days, or maybe a week, but... I just don't have a way to measure it when I'm in it. I'm sorry, I just really don't know.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you feel bright and unstoppable with very little sleep, do you notice that you're doing noticeably more activities or projects than you normally would — like, would someone looking at you over several days in a row see a clear difference in how active or busy you are compared to your usual self?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. When I get like that, I'm doing a lot more than usual. At my busiest, I'll sign up for committees, message people about workshops, start several blog drafts, and make big plans for the garden, all in the same day. I'm just constantly moving and starting things. So, yeah, someone would definitely see a clear difference in how busy I am compared to my normal self.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those bright, energetic periods where you're doing all those extra activities and sleeping very little, do you typically find yourself still in that same energetic state when you go to bed on the third night, or does it usually wear off before that?\n\nPatient: I really wish I could give you a clear answer on that. But... like I mentioned earlier, the days just kind of blur together for me when I get like that. I might still be going strong when I go to bed on the third night, or maybe it starts to fade before then. I just don't keep track of the time well enough to tell you for sure if it's still happening by night three.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those bright, energetic periods where you're doing all those extra activities and sleeping very little, would your husband or someone close to you say they noticed you acting that way for at least three or four days in a row, or does it seem like it would be less than that?\n\nPatient: I've never actually asked my husband to count the days, so... I really don't know what he would say. He definitely notices when I get into those moods because I'm doing so much around the house, but I'm not sure if he'd say it lasts three or four days in a row, or if it's shorter than that. We just haven't ever talked about it in terms of an exact timeframe.\n\nDoctor: Let me ask it a different way. When you're in one of those bright, energetic periods, is there a point where you wake up and think, 'Oh, I'm back to normal now,' or do you find that you keep waking up feeling that same energetic, charged-up way for several days before it finally shifts?\n\nPatient: I know you're trying to pin down the timeframe, but... I really just don't know. I don't think there's a specific morning where I wake up and think, 'Okay, I'm back to normal.' It's not like a switch flips that I notice right away. \n\nI'm just going and going, and then eventually... I'm not. But I couldn't tell you if I keep waking up feeling that charged-up way for several days before it finally shifts. I just don't really pay attention to it like that while it's happening.\n\nDoctor: I know the timing is hard to pin down, but let me ask this way: On a scale where 1 is 'definitely less than 3 days' and 5 is 'definitely 4 or more days in a row' — where would you place those bright, energetic periods where you're sleeping very little and doing all those extra activities? Which number feels closest to what you remember?\n\nPatient: I know you're trying to give me a way to measure it with that scale, but... I really just can't pick a number for you. \n\nBecause I honestly don't know if it's a 1, or a 5, or something in between. Like I've been saying, I just don't have a clear memory of how many days those periods actually last when I'm in them. I would just be completely guessing if I picked a number, and I don't want to tell you the wrong thing. I just really don't know.\n\nDoctor: I understand the days blur together for you during those bright, energetic periods. But let me ask you this: During those times when you're sleeping just a few hours and doing all those extra activities — like the blog posts, committees, and garden plans — would you say you're able to do all of that in a single day, or does it actually take you multiple days to do all those things because you're spreading them out?\n\nPatient: Well, I actually do it all in the same day. I don't really spread it out. \n\nWhen I'm at my busiest, I'll sign up for committees, message people about workshops, start several different blog drafts, and make these big plans for the garden... and that's all just in one single day. I just kind of tackle it all at once.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those bright, energetic periods where you're doing all those extra activities in a single day — like the committees, blog posts, and garden plans — does that energetic, charged-up feeling usually come back the next day as well, or does it tend to go away after that one day?\n\nPatient: I really couldn't tell you if it comes back the next day or not. I know I just said I do all those things in one day, but that doesn't mean I know what happens the day after that. I just... I don't track it. I don't know if I wake up feeling the same way again or if it stops. I'm sorry, I just don't have an answer for that.\n\nDoctor: I know you've said the days blur together, but let me ask it this way: On those days when you're doing all those activities in one day — the committees, blog posts, and garden plans — do you typically feel that same energetic, charged-up feeling the very next morning when you wake up?\n\nPatient: I... I really don't know. Like I said before, I just don't remember what happens the next morning. I know I do all that stuff in one day, but I can't tell you if I wake up feeling that same way the day after. I just don't keep track of it like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
